### Question 117: Neuroscience: Basal Ganglia: Part 3 Focus 17 (pallidus)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the specific structure within the basal ganglia (globus pallidus interna/externa) affected by a lesion based on clinical presentation (hemiballismus/parkinsonism) and its relationship to the subthalamic nucleus (STN) and substantia nigra pars reticulata (SNr). (识别病变对基底神经节内特定结构（苍白球内/外侧部）的影响，基于临床表现（半弹道运动/帕金森综合征）及其与丘脑下核（STN）和黑质网状部（SNr）的关系。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department with acute onset of involuntary, violent, flinging movements of his right arm and leg. He describes feeling like his limbs are "out of control." His wife reports that these movements started suddenly this morning after he woke up. His medical history is significant for hypertension and type 2 diabetes mellitus, both well-controlled. Neurological examination reveals severe choreoathetosis and hemiballismus affecting the right side of his body. Strength is 5/5 bilaterally, but purposeful movements are severely impaired on the right due to the involuntary movements. Sensory examination is normal. Deep tendon reflexes are normal and symmetric. Cranial nerves are intact. MRI of the brain reveals a small, acute lacunar infarct in the left subthalamic nucleus (STN).

Which structure is most likely directly affected by the lesion described, leading to the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Globus Pallidus Externa (GPe)
(B) Globus Pallidus Interna (GPi)
(C) Substantia Nigra Pars Compacta (SNc)
(D) Substantia Nigra Pars Reticulata (SNr)
(E) Caudate Nucleus

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with acute onset of involuntary, violent flinging movements (hemiballismus) affecting the right side. This points towards a lesion affecting the motor control pathways, specifically the basal ganglia circuitry.
- **Neurological Examination:** Severe choreoathetosis and hemiballismus on the right side. Normal strength, sensation, and reflexes. This localizes the problem to the basal ganglia or its projections, rather than the primary motor cortex, corticospinal tract, or peripheral nerves.
- **Imaging Findings:** Acute lacunar infarct in the *left* subthalamic nucleus (STN). The lesion is contralateral to the side of the motor symptoms (right hemiballismus).
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is *most likely directly affected* by the lesion (STN infarct) leading to the symptoms. This requires understanding the functional connections of the STN within the basal ganglia circuitry.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Basal Ganglia Circuitry:** The basal ganglia are a group of subcortical nuclei involved in motor control, learning, and executive functions. Key components include the striatum (caudate and putamen), globus pallidus (GPe and GPi), subthalamic nucleus (STN), and substantia nigra (SNc and SNr).
- **Indirect Pathway:** The indirect pathway originates in the motor cortex and projects to the striatum (caudate and putamen). From the striatum, GABAergic neurons project to the GPe. The GPe then projects via GABAergic neurons to the STN. The STN is glutamatergic and projects to the GPi and SNr. The GPi and SNr are GABAergic and project to the thalamus (specifically the ventral anterior and ventral lateral nuclei), which in turn projects back to the motor cortex.
- **STN Function:** The STN acts as a crucial relay station in the indirect pathway. It receives inhibitory input from the GPe and sends excitatory input to the GPi/SNr.
- **Lesion of STN:** A lesion (like an infarct) in the STN disrupts the indirect pathway. The loss of excitatory drive from the STN to the GPi/SNr leads to *decreased* inhibition of the thalamus. This results in excessive thalamocortical excitation, causing hyperkinetic movements.
- **Hemiballismus:** Hemiballismus is a severe form of hyperkinesia characterized by violent, flinging movements of the limbs, typically contralateral to the lesion. It is classically associated with lesions of the STN or its projections.
- **Relationship between STN and SNr:** The STN projects *to* the SNr. The SNr is functionally similar to the GPi and is the final output nucleus of the indirect pathway, projecting inhibitory signals to the thalamus. Damage to the STN leads to disinhibition of the GPi/SNr, resulting in thalamic overactivity and hyperkinesia. Therefore, the SNr is functionally downstream of the STN in this pathway and is indirectly affected by the STN lesion, contributing to the final output.
- **Other Structures:**
    - GPe: Inhibits STN. Lesion would *decrease* STN activity, potentially leading to hypokinesia (like Parkinsonism).
    - GPi: Inhibits thalamus. Lesion would *decrease* thalamic inhibition, leading to hyperkinesia, but STN lesions are more classic for hemiballismus.
    - SNc: Produces dopamine, projects to striatum. Degeneration causes Parkinsonism (hypokinesia).
    - Caudate Nucleus: Part of the striatum, receives input from cortex, projects to GPe. Lesions can cause various movement disorders, but STN lesions are specific for hemiballismus.

##### 3. Synthesis:
The patient has right hemiballismus due to a left STN infarct. The STN normally excites the GPi and SNr. Damage to the STN removes this excitatory drive, leading to decreased inhibition from the GPi/SNr onto the thalamus. This disinhibition results in excessive thalamocortical activity, causing hyperkinetic movements (hemiballismus). The SNr is a key downstream target of the STN and a critical component of the indirect pathway whose activity is altered by the STN lesion, contributing directly to the motor symptoms.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Globus Pallidus Externa (GPe): The GPe inhibits the STN. A lesion in the GPe would *decrease* the excitatory drive to the GPi/SNr, potentially leading to *hypo*kinetic symptoms (like Parkinsonism), not hyperkinesia like hemiballismus. This is the opposite effect.
- (B) Globus Pallidus Interna (GPi): The GPi inhibits the thalamus. A lesion in the GPi would *decrease* thalamic inhibition, leading to hyperkinesia. While GPi lesions can cause hyperkinesia, the classic cause of hemiballismus is STN damage. Furthermore, the STN projects *to* the GPi, so the GPi is affected *by* the STN lesion, but the SNr is also a direct downstream target and functionally equivalent output nucleus in this pathway. The question asks what is *directly affected* by the STN lesion in terms of causing the symptoms, and the SNr's disinhibition is the final step leading to thalamic overactivity.
- (C) Substantia Nigra Pars Compacta (SNc): The SNc produces dopamine and projects to the striatum. Degeneration of SNc neurons leads to a deficiency of dopamine in the striatum, causing Parkinson's disease (hypokinesia, rigidity, tremor). This is the opposite clinical picture of hemiballismus.
- (E) Caudate Nucleus: The caudate nucleus is part of the striatum. Lesions here can cause various movement disorders (e.g., chorea in Huntington's disease), but they are not the classic cause of hemiballismus associated with STN damage. The caudate receives input from the cortex and projects to the GPe.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Function in Indirect Pathway                                  | Effect of Lesion                                  | Clinical Correlation                                  |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| **STN**              | Excitatory input to GPi/SNr                                 | Decreased inhibition of GPi/SNr -> Thalamus overactivity | **Hemiballismus** (classic)                          |
| GPe                  | Inhibitory input to STN                                      | Decreased excitation of GPi/SNr -> Thalamus hypoactivity | Parkinsonism (hypokinesia)                          |
| GPi                  | Inhibitory output to Thalamus                                | Decreased thalamic inhibition -> Thalamus overactivity | Hyperkinesia (e.g., chorea, dystonia)                |
| **SNr**              | Inhibitory output to Thalamus (functionally similar to GPi) | Decreased thalamic inhibition -> Thalamus overactivity | Hyperkinesia (e.g., chorea, dystonia, hemiballismus) |
| SNc                  | Dopamine production, projects to Striatum                    | Dopamine deficiency in Striatum                    | Parkinsonism (hypokinesia, rigidity, tremor)         |
| Caudate Nucleus      | Input from Cortex, projects to GPe                            | Variable, often chorea/dystonia                   | Huntington's disease, other movement disorders        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hemiballismus is classically caused by lesions of the contralateral subthalamic nucleus (STN) or its projections. The STN is a key component of the indirect pathway, exciting the GPi and SNr. Damage to the STN leads to disinhibition of the thalamus via the GPi/SNr, resulting in thalamocortical overactivity and hyperkinetic movements. Differentiate hemiballismus (STN lesion) from Parkinsonism (SNc or GPe lesion) and chorea (lesions affecting the striatum or GPi/SNr).

<a id='question-118'></a>

### Question 118: Neuroscience: Basal Ganglia: Part 3 Focus 18 (Putamen)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Localization of basal ganglia lesions, specifically putaminal infarcts, and their associated clinical manifestations, including contralateral hemiparesis, hemisensory loss, and potential cognitive/behavioral changes. (定位基底神经节病灶，特别是壳核梗死，及其相关的临床表现，包括对侧偏瘫、偏感觉丧失以及潜在的认知/行为改变。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with acute onset of right-sided weakness and numbness. He reports that the symptoms started suddenly while he was eating breakfast. On examination, his vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals significant weakness in the right upper and lower extremities (strength 2/5), decreased sensation to light touch and pinprick on the right side of his body, and mild dysarthria. Reflexes are brisk on the right side. Cranial nerves are intact. He is alert and oriented but exhibits some difficulty with rapid alternating movements on the right side. A non-contrast head CT scan shows a hypodense lesion in the left basal ganglia region, specifically involving the lateral portion of the putamen extending towards the internal capsule.

Which anatomical structure is most likely affected by this lesion, leading to the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Globus Pallidus Internus (GPi)
(B) Substantia Nigra Pars Compacta (SNc)
(C) Subthalamic Nucleus (STN)
(D) Putamen
(E) Caudate Nucleus

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases the likelihood of an ischemic event (stroke).
- **Onset:** Acute onset of right-sided weakness and numbness suggests a vascular event affecting the contralateral (left) side of the brain.
- **Symptoms:** Right-sided weakness (hemiparesis) and numbness (hemisensory loss) indicate involvement of the corticospinal tract and thalamocortical sensory pathways. Dysarthria suggests involvement of motor control pathways. Difficulty with rapid alternating movements points to cerebellar or basal ganglia dysfunction.
- **Neurological Exam:** Contralateral (right-sided) motor and sensory deficits are key localizing signs. Brisk reflexes on the right side are consistent with an upper motor neuron lesion.
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast head CT shows a hypodense lesion (indicating ischemia/infarction) in the left basal ganglia, specifically the lateral putamen extending towards the internal capsule.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the anatomical structure most likely affected.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Basal Ganglia Anatomy:** The basal ganglia are a group of subcortical nuclei involved in motor control, learning, executive functions, and emotions. Key components include the striatum (caudate nucleus and putamen), globus pallidus (internal and external segments), substantia nigra (pars compacta and pars reticulata), and subthalamic nucleus.
- **Putamen:** The putamen is the lateral part of the striatum. It receives input from the cerebral cortex (especially motor, premotor, and somatosensory areas) and projects to the globus pallidus and substantia nigra pars reticulata. The putamen is crucial for motor planning and execution.
- **Location of Lesion:** The CT scan localizes the lesion to the left putamen extending towards the internal capsule. The internal capsule contains descending corticospinal tracts (motor) and ascending sensory tracts (thalamocortical).
- **Clinical Correlation:** A lesion in the putamen, especially when extending into the adjacent internal capsule, directly damages the corticospinal and thalamocortical tracts. This explains the contralateral (right-sided) hemiparesis and hemisensory loss. The putamen itself is involved in motor control, contributing to the dysarthria and difficulty with rapid alternating movements.
- **Embryology:** The putamen develops from the dorsal part of the telencephalon.
- **Vascular Supply:** The putamen is primarily supplied by branches of the middle cerebral artery (MCA), particularly the lenticulostriate arteries. This aligns with the patient's vascular risk factors and the presentation of an acute ischemic stroke.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Globus Pallidus Internus (GPi):** The GPi is the primary output nucleus of the basal ganglia, projecting to the thalamus. Lesions here typically cause contralateral hemiballismus (violent, involuntary flinging movements) or dystonia, not the hemiparesis and hemisensory loss seen in this patient. It is medial to the putamen.
- (B) **Substantia Nigra Pars Compacta (SNc):** The SNc is located in the midbrain and is the primary source of dopamine for the basal ganglia. Degeneration of SNc neurons causes Parkinson's disease, characterized by bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and postural instability. A stroke affecting the SNc is rare and would not typically present with acute hemiparesis and hemisensory loss.
- (C) **Subthalamic Nucleus (STN):** The STN is located ventral to the thalamus and is part of the indirect pathway of the basal ganglia. Lesions here typically cause contralateral hemiballismus, similar to GPi lesions. It is located inferior to the caudate nucleus and putamen.
- (E) **Caudate Nucleus:** The caudate nucleus is the medial part of the striatum. While lesions can cause motor deficits, caudate lesions are more strongly associated with cognitive and behavioral changes (e.g., apathy, executive dysfunction) and chorea (especially Huntington's disease). Although adjacent to the putamen, the primary motor and sensory deficits described are more characteristic of a putamen/internal capsule lesion.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location                                  | Primary Function(s)                                  | Typical Lesion Symptoms                                  | Relationship to Putamen |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|------------------------|
| **Putamen (Correct)** | Lateral basal ganglia                     | Motor planning, execution, sensory integration        | Contralateral hemiparesis, hemisensory loss, dysarthria | Adjacent to Globus Pallidus |
| Globus Pallidus Internus | Medial to Putamen, ventral to Caudate     | Output nucleus to thalamus                            | Contralateral hemiballismus, dystonia                   | Medial                 |
| Substantia Nigra Pars Compacta | Midbrain                                | Dopamine production                                   | Parkinsonism (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor)           | Not in basal ganglia    |
| Subthalamic Nucleus   | Ventral thalamus, inferior to Caudate/Putamen | Part of indirect pathway                             | Contralateral hemiballismus                             | Inferior               |
| Caudate Nucleus       | Medial basal ganglia                     | Motor control, cognition, emotion, procedural learning | Cognitive/behavioral changes, chorea, motor deficits     | Medial                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
A lesion in the putamen, particularly extending into the internal capsule, classically causes contralateral hemiparesis and hemisensory loss due to damage to the corticospinal and thalamocortical tracts. Differentiating putaminal lesions from lesions in other basal ganglia structures (GPi, STN - hemiballismus; SNc - Parkinsonism; Caudate - cognitive/behavioral changes, chorea) relies on understanding the specific functions and connections of each nucleus. Remember the vascular supply (MCA branches) for putaminal strokes.

<a id='question-119'></a>

### Question 119: Neuroscience: Basal Ganglia: Part 3 Focus 19 (ventricle)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the anatomical structure affected by a lesion causing hydrocephalus and contralateral hemiparesis, specifically relating to the basal ganglia and ventricular system. (鉴别基底神经节和脑室系统病变导致的脑积水和对侧偏瘫的解剖结构)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-month history of progressive gait disturbance, difficulty with fine motor skills, and recent onset of episodes of urinary incontinence. His past medical history is significant for hypertension and hyperlipidemia. Neurological examination reveals bradykinesia, rigidity, and a resting tremor predominantly on the right side. Cognitive testing shows mild executive dysfunction. Cranial nerve examination is normal. Motor examination reveals moderate right-sided hemiparesis. Sensory examination is intact. Deep tendon reflexes are hyperactive bilaterally, with Babinski signs present on the right. An MRI of the brain reveals enlargement of the lateral ventricles, particularly the right lateral ventricle, with evidence of obstructive hydrocephalus. The obstruction appears to be located at the level of the foramen of Monro, with a mass lesion identified in the right lateral ventricle, compressing the adjacent structures. The lesion is isointense on T1-weighted images and hyperintense on T2-weighted images, with heterogeneous enhancement after gadolinium administration. Which structure is most likely involved in this patient's presentation?

(A) Substantia nigra pars compacta
(B) Globus pallidus internus (GPi)
(C) Subthalamic nucleus (STN)
(D) Caudate nucleus head
(E) Putamen

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia).
- **Symptoms:** Progressive gait disturbance, fine motor difficulty, urinary incontinence (suggesting frontal lobe/basal ganglia dysfunction), right-sided parkinsonism (bradykinesia, rigidity, resting tremor), mild executive dysfunction.
- **Neurological Exam:** Right hemiparesis, hyperreflexia, Babinski sign (upper motor neuron signs).
- **Imaging:** MRI shows right lateral ventricle enlargement, obstructive hydrocephalus at the foramen of Monro, mass lesion in the right lateral ventricle compressing adjacent structures.
- **Key Clue:** The location of the mass lesion within the right lateral ventricle, specifically causing obstruction at the foramen of Monro and compressing adjacent structures, combined with the clinical presentation of parkinsonism and hemiparesis. The foramen of Monro is the opening between the lateral ventricles and the third ventricle. Structures adjacent to the foramen of Monro within the lateral ventricle include the head of the caudate nucleus and the posterior limb of the internal capsule. Compression of the head of the caudate nucleus and/or the posterior limb of the internal capsule can explain the symptoms. The parkinsonian features (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor) point towards involvement of the basal ganglia circuitry. The hemiparesis points towards involvement of the corticospinal tract in the posterior limb of the internal capsule. The location of the mass within the lateral ventricle, specifically near the foramen of Monro, strongly implicates the head of the caudate nucleus.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Lateral Ventricle Anatomy:** The lateral ventricles are C-shaped cavities within the cerebral hemispheres. The anterior horn extends into the frontal lobe, the body into the parietal lobe, and the posterior horn into the temporal lobe. The head of the caudate nucleus forms the medial wall of the anterior horn of the lateral ventricle. The foramen of Monro connects the lateral ventricles to the third ventricle.
- **Basal Ganglia Anatomy:** The basal ganglia are a group of subcortical nuclei involved in motor control, learning, executive functions, and emotions. Key components include the striatum (caudate nucleus and putamen), globus pallidus (internal and external segments), subthalamic nucleus, and substantia nigra.
- **Caudate Nucleus:** The caudate nucleus has a head, body, and tail. The head is located anteriorly, forming the medial wall of the anterior horn of the lateral ventricle and is continuous with the ventral striatum. The body is located superior to the thalamus, and the tail curves around the thalamus to join the putamen, forming the ventral striatum. Lesions of the caudate nucleus, particularly the head, can cause parkinsonian symptoms (due to its role in the indirect pathway) and cognitive/behavioral changes. Compression of the head of the caudate nucleus by a mass in the anterior horn of the lateral ventricle can lead to these symptoms.
- **Foramen of Monro Obstruction:** Obstruction at the foramen of Monro prevents CSF flow from the lateral ventricles into the third ventricle, leading to enlargement of the lateral ventricles (hydrocephalus) proximal to the obstruction.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (parkinsonism, hemiparesis, cognitive changes) and imaging findings (hydrocephalus due to obstruction at the foramen of Monro by a mass in the right lateral ventricle) are consistent with a lesion affecting the head of the caudate nucleus and potentially compressing the posterior limb of the internal capsule. The parkinsonian features are often associated with lesions affecting the indirect pathway components, including the caudate nucleus and subthalamic nucleus. The hemiparesis is likely due to compression of the corticospinal tract in the posterior limb of the internal capsule.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Substantia nigra pars compacta:** Located in the midbrain, primarily involved in dopamine production. Degeneration causes Parkinson's disease. A lesion here would not typically cause obstructive hydrocephalus at the foramen of Monro or directly compress the structures in the lateral ventricle.
- (B) **Globus pallidus internus (GPi):** Part of the basal ganglia, located medial to the globus pallidus externus and lateral to the subthalamic nucleus. It is an output nucleus of the basal ganglia. Lesions here can cause hemiballismus or parkinsonism, but it is not located within the lateral ventricle near the foramen of Monro.
- (C) **Subthalamic nucleus (STN):** Part of the basal ganglia, located inferior to the thalamus and medial to the internal capsule. Lesions here typically cause hemiballismus. It is not located within the lateral ventricle near the foramen of Monro.
- (E) **Putamen:** Part of the striatum (ventral striatum), located lateral to the globus pallidus and medial to the external capsule. Lesions here can cause parkinsonism or dystonia. While part of the basal ganglia, it is not located within the lateral ventricle forming the medial wall of the anterior horn near the foramen of Monro. The putamen is part of the lentiform nucleus, which is lateral to the internal capsule.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                                                                 | Relation to Lateral Ventricle/Foramen of Monro | Clinical Manifestations (Lesion)                                  |
| :------------------------ | :----------------------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Caudate Nucleus Head (D)** | Medial wall of anterior horn of lateral ventricle, adjacent to foramen of Monro | Forms part of the wall, near obstruction site | Parkinsonism, cognitive changes, hydrocephalus (if obstructing) |
| Substantia Nigra (A)      | Midbrain                                                                 | Not related                                    | Parkinsonism (degeneration)                                     |
| Globus Pallidus Internus (B) | Medial to GP externus, lateral to STN                                   | Not related                                    | Hemiballismus, parkinsonism                                      |
| Subthalamic Nucleus (C)   | Inferior to thalamus, medial to internal capsule                          | Not related                                    | Hemiballismus                                                     |
| Putamen (E)               | Lateral to GP, medial to external capsule                                 | Not related                                    | Parkinsonism, dystonia                                           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The head of the caudate nucleus forms the medial wall of the anterior horn of the lateral ventricle, adjacent to the foramen of Monro. A mass lesion in this location can obstruct CSF flow, causing hydrocephalus, and compress the caudate nucleus, leading to parkinsonian symptoms and cognitive changes. Differentiating basal ganglia structures based on their location relative to the ventricular system and internal capsule is crucial. Remember the caudate head's specific relationship to the anterior horn and foramen of Monro.

<a id='question-120'></a>

### Question 120: Neuroscience: Basal Ganglia: Part 3 Focus 20 (Figure)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the basal ganglia, specifically the location of the globus pallidus interna (GPi) relative to the optic tract and posterior cerebral artery (PCA), and the clinical consequences of lesions affecting the GPi or surrounding structures. (理解基底神经节的解剖关系，特别是内侧苍白球 (GPi) 相对于视神经和后脑动脉 (PCA) 的位置，以及影响 GPi 或周围结构的病变产生的临床后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension presents to the emergency department with sudden onset of right-sided hemiparesis and contralateral homonymous hemianopia. He also reports difficulty initiating voluntary movements on the right side and exhibits mild bradykinesia and rigidity. Non-contrast CT scan of the head reveals a small, acute lacunar infarct in the left internal capsule. MRI performed 24 hours later shows a lesion extending into the left globus pallidus interna (GPi). The patient's symptoms persist despite supportive care. Which of the following structures is most likely compromised by the extension of the infarct into the GPi?

(A) Optic tract
(B) Substantia nigra pars compacta
(C) Posterior cerebral artery (PCA)
(D) Internal capsule (posterior limb)
(E) Subthalamic nucleus

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, hypertension, sudden right hemiparesis, left homonymous hemianopia, difficulty initiating movement (right), bradykinesia, rigidity. This points towards a left-sided lesion affecting motor pathways and potentially the visual pathway. The bradykinesia and rigidity suggest involvement of the basal ganglia.
- **Imaging Findings:** Lacunar infarct in the left internal capsule extending into the left GPi. This localizes the lesion anatomically.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is *most likely compromised* by the extension into the GPi. This requires knowledge of the GPi's anatomical neighbors.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Location of GPi:** The globus pallidus interna (GPi) is a major component of the basal ganglia, located medial to the globus pallidus externa (GPe) and lateral to the internal capsule. It is situated anterior to the thalamus and posterior to the lentiform nucleus (putamen + GPe).
- **Key Anatomical Relationships:** Crucially, the GPi lies *anterior* to the posterior limb of the internal capsule. The optic tract courses *posterior* to the GPi, running between the GPi and the posterior cerebral artery (PCA). The PCA itself runs superiorly and posteriorly, often closely related to the posterior aspect of the GPi and the optic tract. The substantia nigra pars compacta is located more inferiorly and medially, in the midbrain, near the cerebral peduncle. The subthalamic nucleus is located inferior to the thalamus and lateral to the substantia nigra. The internal capsule (posterior limb) is lateral to the GPi.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The initial infarct in the internal capsule explains the hemiparesis. The extension into the GPi explains the bradykinesia and rigidity (basal ganglia signs). The question asks what *else* might be compromised by the GPi lesion. Given the close proximity, the optic tract, which runs posterior to the GPi, is vulnerable to extension of a lesion from the GPi. Damage to the optic tract causes contralateral homonymous hemianopia, which the patient exhibits. The PCA runs nearby, but the optic tract is more directly posterior to the GPi. The substantia nigra and subthalamic nucleus are located in the midbrain, not directly adjacent to the GPi in the basal ganglia region. The posterior limb of the internal capsule is lateral to the GPi, not posterior. Therefore, the optic tract is the most likely structure compromised by extension of the infarct into the GPi, explaining the patient's visual field defect.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Optic tract:** This is the correct answer. The optic tract runs immediately posterior to the GPi. An infarct extending posteriorly from the GPi can easily involve the optic tract, leading to contralateral homonymous hemianopia, which is present in the patient.
- (B): **Substantia nigra pars compacta:** This structure is located in the midbrain, ventral to the substantia nigra pars reticulata, and is not directly adjacent to the GPi. Lesions here cause Parkinsonism, but are not typically affected by infarcts extending from the GPi.
- (C): **Posterior cerebral artery (PCA):** The PCA runs superior and posterior to the GPi and optic tract. While close, the optic tract is more immediately posterior to the GPi. Infarcts affecting the PCA typically cause visual cortex ischemia (contralateral homonymous hemianopia) or thalamic/occipital lobe symptoms, but the optic tract is more vulnerable to direct extension from the GPi.
- (D): **Internal capsule (posterior limb):** The posterior limb of the internal capsule is lateral to the GPi. The initial infarct was in the internal capsule, causing hemiparesis. Extension of the GPi lesion would be anterior or posterior, not lateral, to the GPi.
- (E): **Subthalamic nucleus:** This nucleus is located inferior to the thalamus and lateral to the substantia nigra in the midbrain. It is not directly adjacent to the GPi. Lesions here cause hemiballismus.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location Relative to GPi | Clinical Deficit (if lesioned)                                  | Embryological Origin (Germ Layer) |
| :------------------------ | :----------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Optic Tract (Correct)** | Posterior                | Contralateral Homonymous Hemianopia                             | Neuroectoderm                     |
| Substantia Nigra          | Inferior/Medial (Midbrain) | Parkinsonism (loss of dopaminergic neurons)                     | Neuroectoderm                     |
| PCA                       | Superior/Posterior       | Visual Cortex Infarct (Homonymous Hemianopia), Thalamic Infarct | Mesoderm (smooth muscle/endothelium) |
| Internal Capsule          | Lateral                  | Hemiparesis/Hemisensory Loss (depending on limb)                 | Neural Crest/Neuroectoderm        |
| Subthalamic Nucleus       | Inferior (Midbrain)       | Hemiballismus                                                  | Neuroectoderm                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The GPi is located in the basal ganglia, anterior to the posterior limb of the internal capsule and medial to the GPe. The optic tract runs immediately posterior to the GPi, making it vulnerable to lesions extending posteriorly from the GPi. Remember the key relationships: GPi is medial to the internal capsule, lateral to the thalamus, anterior to the optic tract, and superior to the substantia nigra (which is in the midbrain). Differentiating the location of the optic tract, PCA, internal capsule, substantia nigra, and subthalamic nucleus relative to the GPi is crucial for localizing neurological deficits.

<a id='question-121'></a>

### Question 121: Neuroscience: Basal Ganglia: Part 3 Focus 21 (Horizontal)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical presentation resulting from lesions affecting the horizontal limb (internal capsule) of the basal ganglia, specifically differentiating between lesions affecting the caudate nucleus/putamen versus the globus pallidus/substantia nigra pars reticulata, and their impact on motor control (e.g., hemiballismus vs. contralateral hemiparesis). (理解基底神经节水平肢（内囊）病变导致的临床表现，特别是区分纹状体（尾状核/壳核）与苍白球/黑质网状部病变，及其对运动控制的影响，例如半弹道运动障碍与对侧偏瘫。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department with acute onset of involuntary, violent, flinging movements of his right arm and leg. The movements are primarily proximal and affect the limbs on one side of his body. He reports that the movements started suddenly this morning after he woke up. His medical history is significant for hypertension and type 2 diabetes mellitus, both poorly controlled. Neurological examination reveals hypertonia and hyperreflexia in the right limbs, along with the characteristic flinging movements. Sensory examination and cranial nerve function are intact. Brain imaging (MRI) shows a small, acute lacunar infarct in the left internal capsule, specifically involving the area adjacent to the globus pallidus interna (GPi) and substantia nigra pars reticulata (SNr).

Which of the following structures is MOST likely affected by the infarct, leading to the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Caudate nucleus (尾状核)
(B) Putamen (壳核)
(C) Globus pallidus interna (GPi) (内侧苍白球)
(D) Substantia nigra pars reticulata (SNr) (黑质网状部)
(E) Subthalamic nucleus (STN) (壳下核)

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with acute onset of involuntary, violent, flinging movements (hemiballismus) of the right arm and leg. This is a classic presentation of hemiballismus.
- **Medical History:** Hypertension and poorly controlled diabetes are risk factors for lacunar infarcts.
- **Neurological Examination:** Hypertonia and hyperreflexia in the right limbs, consistent with upper motor neuron signs. The flinging movements are the hallmark of hemiballismus.
- **Imaging:** MRI shows a lacunar infarct in the left internal capsule, adjacent to the GPi and SNr. This localizes the lesion to the basal ganglia circuitry.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific structure affected by the infarct causing hemiballismus.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Basal Ganglia Circuitry:** The basal ganglia are involved in motor control, planning, and execution. The indirect pathway normally inhibits the thalamus, preventing unwanted movements. The direct pathway facilitates thalamic activity, promoting desired movements.
- **Hemiballismus:** This syndrome is characterized by violent, involuntary flinging movements of the limbs, typically contralateral to the lesion. It results from damage to the subthalamic nucleus (STN) or its projections.
- **STN Function:** The STN is a key component of the indirect pathway. It sends excitatory (glutamatergic) projections to the GPi and SNr. Damage to the STN removes this excitatory drive.
- **Effect of STN Lesion:** Loss of STN input leads to decreased inhibition of the thalamus by the GPi/SNr. This disinhibition results in excessive thalamocortical drive, causing hyperkinesia (involuntary movements) like hemiballismus.
- **Anatomical Location:** The STN is located ventral to the thalamus, medial to the internal capsule, and superior to the substantia nigra. Lacunar infarcts in the internal capsule can affect structures adjacent to it, including the STN. The vignette specifically mentions the infarct is adjacent to the GPi and SNr, placing it in close proximity to the STN, which lies just medial to these structures and ventral to the thalamus.
- **Differential Diagnosis:**
    - **Hemiparesis/Hemiplegia:** Caused by lesions affecting the corticospinal tract (e.g., in the internal capsule, brainstem, or spinal cord). Presents with weakness, spasticity, and hyperreflexia.
    - **Hemichorea:** Similar to hemiballismus but movements are less violent and more flowing. Often associated with lesions of the contralateral putamen or caudate nucleus.
    - **Parkinsonism:** Caused by degeneration of dopaminergic neurons in the substantia nigra pars compacta, leading to bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and postural instability.
    - **Huntington's Disease:** Degeneration of the caudate nucleus and putamen, leading to chorea, cognitive decline, and psychiatric symptoms.
- **Embryology:** The basal ganglia nuclei develop from the telencephalon (caudate, putamen, globus pallidus) and midbrain (substantia nigra, STN). The STN is derived from the ventral part of the diencephalon.
- **Internal Capsule:** A white matter structure containing ascending and descending tracts, including corticospinal tract (anterior limb), thalamocortical radiations (posterior limb), and fibers connecting basal ganglia nuclei (e.g., GPi/SNr to thalamus). Lacunar infarcts commonly occur here due to end-arterial circulation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Caudate nucleus (尾状核):** Lesions of the caudate nucleus are associated with chorea (often seen in Huntington's disease) or cognitive/behavioral changes. Damage typically affects the indirect pathway, leading to reduced inhibition of the thalamus, but the classic presentation is chorea, not hemiballismus.
- (B) **Putamen (壳核):** Lesions of the putamen, often involving the internal capsule adjacent to it, typically cause contralateral hemiparesis (due to corticospinal tract involvement) or hemichorea (due to disruption of the indirect pathway). It is part of the striatum (caudate + putamen).
- (C) **Globus pallidus interna (GPi) (内侧苍白球):** The GPi is the main output nucleus of the basal ganglia, sending inhibitory projections to the thalamus. Lesions of the GPi cause contralateral hemiballismus or chorea due to disinhibition of the thalamus. While the infarct is adjacent to the GPi, the classic lesion causing hemiballismus is the STN. A GPi lesion *can* cause hemiballismus, but the STN is the more common and classic cause, especially with lacunar infarcts in this location. The question asks for the *most likely* structure. The STN is located just medial to the GPi and is frequently affected by small infarcts in this region.
- (D) **Substantia nigra pars reticulata (SNr) (黑质网状部):** The SNr is another output nucleus of the basal ganglia, also sending inhibitory projections to the thalamus. Similar to GPi lesions, damage to the SNr can cause contralateral hemiballismus or chorea. Like the GPi, it's adjacent to the infarct site, but the STN is the primary structure implicated in lacunar hemiballismus in this anatomical region.
- (E) **Subthalamic nucleus (STN) (壳下核):** The STN is a crucial part of the indirect pathway, providing excitatory input to the GPi/SNr. Lesions of the STN remove this excitatory drive, leading to decreased inhibition of the thalamus and resulting in hemiballismus. Lacunar infarcts affecting the internal capsule are a common cause of STN lesions, fitting the clinical presentation and imaging findings described. The location described (adjacent to GPi/SNr in the internal capsule) is precisely where the STN lies.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location                                  | Primary Function in Basal Ganglia Circuitry | Typical Lesion Resulting in Hemiballismus | Other Common Lesion Results                                  |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|
| Subthalamic Nucleus (STN) | Ventral thalamus, medial internal capsule | Excitatory input to GPi/SNr (Indirect Pathway) | **Classic cause of Hemiballismus**        | None specific (often lacunar infarcts)                       |
| Globus Pallidus interna (GPi) | Medial basal ganglia, lateral internal capsule | Inhibitory output to Thalamus (Indirect/Direct) | Can cause Hemiballismus/Chorea           | Hemiparesis (if corticospinal tract involved), Parkinsonism |
| Substantia Nigra pars reticulata (SNr) | Midbrain, medial basal ganglia | Inhibitory output to Thalamus (Indirect/Direct) | Can cause Hemiballismus/Chorea           | Parkinsonism (SNc), Hemiparesis (if adjacent tracts involved) |
| Putamen              | Lateral basal ganglia, lateral internal capsule | Input from Cortex (Indirect/Direct)         | Hemichorea                              | Hemiparesis (if corticospinal tract involved)               |
| Caudate Nucleus      | Medial basal ganglia, superior internal capsule | Input from Cortex (Indirect/Direct)         | Chorea (e.g., Huntington's)             | Cognitive/Behavioral changes                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hemiballismus is most commonly caused by a lesion of the contralateral subthalamic nucleus (STN). Lacunar infarcts in the internal capsule affecting the STN are a frequent etiology. Differentiating hemiballismus from hemichorea, hemiparesis, and Parkinsonism requires understanding the specific basal ganglia circuitry and the function of each nucleus. Remember the STN's excitatory role in the indirect pathway; its loss leads to thalamic disinhibition and hyperkinesia (hemiballismus).

<a id='question-122'></a>

### Question 122: Neuroscience: Basal Ganglia: Part 3 Focus 22 (or Axial)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the basal ganglia, specifically the location of the axial (or Part 3 Focus 22) region, its components (including the globus pallidus interna/externa, putamen, caudate nucleus tail), and the clinical manifestations resulting from lesions in this area, particularly related to motor control and eye movements. (理解基底神经节的解剖关系，特别是轴向区（或Part 3 Focus 22）的位置、组成部分（包括内/外苍白球、壳核、尾状核），以及该区域病变导致的临床表现，特别是与运动控制和眼球运动相关的表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department with acute onset of involuntary, rapid, repetitive eye movements (nystagmus) predominantly affecting his right eye, along with difficulty initiating voluntary movements of his right arm and leg. His past medical history is significant for hypertension and hyperlipidemia. Neurological examination reveals right-sided gaze-evoked nystagmus, slow saccades to the right, and mild right-sided bradykinesia and rigidity. MRI of the brain shows a small, acute lacunar infarct in the left internal capsule, specifically involving the region adjacent to the globus pallidus interna and caudate nucleus tail. Which structure is most likely affected by this infarct, leading to the patient's symptoms?

(A) Substantia nigra pars compacta
(B) Subthalamic nucleus
(C) Globus pallidus externa
(D) Caudate nucleus head
(E) Putamen

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with acute onset right-sided nystagmus, gaze-evoked nystagmus, slow saccades, bradykinesia, and rigidity. This constellation points towards a lesion affecting motor control pathways and potentially structures involved in eye movement control.
- **Past Medical History:** Hypertension and hyperlipidemia are risk factors for lacunar infarcts.
- **Neurological Examination:** Right-sided gaze-evoked nystagmus and slow saccades indicate dysfunction in the neural circuits controlling horizontal eye movements, often involving the frontal eye fields, paramedian pontine reticular formation (PPRF), or structures within the basal ganglia influencing these pathways. Right-sided bradykinesia and rigidity suggest involvement of the motor circuitry, specifically the basal ganglia or related pathways. The laterality (right-sided motor symptoms) suggests a lesion in the contralateral (left) hemisphere.
- **MRI Findings:** Acute lacunar infarct in the left internal capsule, adjacent to the globus pallidus interna and caudate nucleus tail. This localizes the lesion anatomically. The internal capsule contains ascending sensory tracts, descending motor tracts (corticospinal), and projection fibers connecting the cortex to the thalamus and basal ganglia. The proximity to the globus pallidus interna and caudate tail is crucial.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is most likely affected, linking the clinical symptoms (eye movement abnormalities and parkinsonism) to the MRI findings.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Basal Ganglia Anatomy:** The basal ganglia are a group of subcortical nuclei involved in motor control, learning, executive functions, and emotions. Key components include the striatum (caudate nucleus and putamen), globus pallidus (interna and externa), subthalamic nucleus, and substantia nigra.
- **Internal Capsule:** The internal capsule is a white matter structure separating the thalamus and caudate nucleus from the lentiform nucleus (putamen and globus pallidus) and internal medullary lamina. It contains major ascending and descending tracts.
- **Location of Lesion:** The infarct is in the left internal capsule, adjacent to the globus pallidus interna (GPi) and the tail of the caudate nucleus. The putamen is immediately lateral to the GPi and the internal capsule. The caudate tail curves around the thalamus and is closely related to the internal capsule in this region.
- **Clinical Correlation:**
    - **Eye Movement Abnormalities:** Lesions affecting the basal ganglia, particularly the putamen and globus pallidus, can disrupt the neural circuits controlling eye movements, leading to nystagmus, slow saccades, and gaze palsies. The putamen receives input from the cortex and projects to the GPi, which in turn projects to the thalamus, influencing frontal eye fields. Damage here can impair voluntary saccadic eye movements.
    - **Parkinsonism (Bradykinesia, Rigidity):** The basal ganglia play a critical role in motor control. The "indirect pathway" involves the cortex -> striatum (caudate/putamen) -> GPe -> STN -> GPi -> thalamus -> cortex. The "direct pathway" involves cortex -> striatum -> GPi -> thalamus -> cortex. Damage to the putamen or GPi can disrupt these pathways, leading to parkinsonian symptoms.
- **Why Putamen (E)?** The putamen is located laterally within the lentiform nucleus, immediately adjacent to the internal capsule. A lacunar infarct in the internal capsule near the GPi and caudate tail would very likely involve the putamen. Putaminal lesions are well-known to cause both parkinsonian features (bradykinesia, rigidity) and eye movement abnormalities (nystagmus, slow saccades).
- **Why not others?**
    - (A) Substantia nigra pars compacta: Located in the midbrain, primarily involved in dopamine production. Lesions cause Parkinson's disease, but not typically acute lacunar infarcts in the internal capsule.
    - (B) Subthalamic nucleus: Located ventral to the thalamus, part of the indirect pathway. Lesions cause hemiballismus, not the described symptoms.
    - (C) Globus pallidus externa: Part of the indirect pathway. Lesions are less common and typically cause different motor deficits. The infarct is described as adjacent to the GPi, not GPe.
    - (D) Caudate nucleus head: Located anteriorly, part of the striatum. While the tail is involved, the head is less likely to be affected by an infarct in the internal capsule near the GPi. Lesions typically cause cognitive/behavioral changes or chorea.

##### 3. Embryological Origin:
- The basal ganglia nuclei develop from the telencephalon (forebrain). The striatum (caudate and putamen) develops from the dorsal telencephalon, while the pallidum develops from the ventral telencephalon. The internal capsule forms as the white matter separating these structures.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Substantia nigra pars compacta: Incorrect. This structure is located in the midbrain, not the internal capsule. Lesions here cause Parkinson's disease due to dopamine depletion, characterized by tremor, rigidity, bradykinesia, and postural instability, but not typically acute lacunar infarcts presenting with prominent eye movement abnormalities alongside parkinsonism in this specific location. The vignette describes an internal capsule infarct.
- (B) Subthalamic nucleus: Incorrect. This structure is located ventral to the thalamus, medial to the internal capsule. Lesions typically cause hemiballismus (violent, flinging movements), which is not described in the vignette. The location described in the vignette (internal capsule adjacent to GPi/caudate tail) does not directly involve the STN.
- (C) Globus pallidus externa: Incorrect. While the GPi is mentioned as adjacent, the GPe is located dorsally to the GPi. Lesions of the GPe are less common and typically cause different motor deficits. The putamen is more immediately adjacent to the internal capsule in the described location and is strongly associated with the combination of parkinsonism and eye movement abnormalities seen here.
- (D) Caudate nucleus head: Incorrect. The caudate nucleus head is located anteriorly, superior to the putamen and GPi. The infarct is described near the GPi and the *tail* of the caudate nucleus, which curves posteriorly around the thalamus. The head is less likely to be involved in this specific location. Lesions of the head are more associated with cognitive and behavioral changes.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location Relative to Internal Capsule | Key Clinical Features of Lesion                                  | Embryological Origin |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Putamen (Correct)** | Lateral to GPi, adjacent to internal capsule | Parkinsonism (bradykinesia, rigidity), eye movement abnormalities | Telencephalon (dorsal) |
| Substantia Nigra     | Midbrain                               | Parkinsonism (tremor, rigidity, bradykinesia)                      | Mesencephalon        |
| Subthalamic Nucleus  | Ventral to thalamus, medial to IC      | Hemiballismus                                                     | Telencephalon (ventral)|
| Globus Pallidus Externa| Dorsal to GPi                         | Less common, variable motor deficits                             | Telencephalon (ventral)|
| Caudate Head         | Anterior, superior to putamen/GPi      | Cognitive/behavioral changes, chorea                             | Telencephalon (dorsal) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Lacunar infarcts in the internal capsule can affect adjacent basal ganglia structures. Lesions involving the putamen and globus pallidus often present with a combination of parkinsonian features (bradykinesia, rigidity) and eye movement abnormalities (nystagmus, slow saccades). Differentiating basal ganglia syndromes requires precise anatomical localization. Key differentials include Parkinson's disease (SNc), hemiballismus (STN), and lesions affecting specific pathways within the basal ganglia circuitry.

<a id='question-123'></a>

### Question 123: Neuroscience: Basal Ganglia: Part 3 Focus 23 (Section)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the anatomical relationships within the basal ganglia, specifically the internal capsule and its vulnerability to vascular events, leading to specific motor deficits. (理解基底神经节内的解剖关系，特别是内囊的脆弱性，以及血管事件如何导致特定的运动功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia presents to the emergency department with sudden onset of right-sided weakness and sensory loss. The weakness predominantly affects the right face, arm, and leg, with the arm and face being more affected than the leg. He also exhibits right-sided sensory loss affecting the face, arm, and leg, again more pronounced in the face and arm. His speech is slurred (dysarthria), and he has difficulty swallowing (dysphagia). Neurological examination reveals right hemiparesis (face > arm > leg), right hemisensory loss (face > arm > leg), and right lower facial droop. Reflexes are brisk on the right side. An emergent non-contrast CT scan of the head reveals a small, acute ischemic infarct in the left internal capsule.

Which of the following structures is MOST likely affected by the infarct, leading to this specific clinical presentation?

(A) Putamen
(B) Globus Pallidus
(C) Substantia Nigra
(D) Internal Capsule (Posterior Limb)
(E) Caudate Nucleus

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with vascular risk factors (hypertension, hyperlipidemia). This increases the likelihood of a stroke.
- **Onset:** Sudden onset of right-sided neurological deficits. This is characteristic of a vascular event (stroke).
- **Symptoms:** Right-sided weakness (hemiparesis) and sensory loss (hemianesthesia) affecting the face, arm, and leg. The face and arm are more affected than the leg. Dysarthria and dysphagia are also present.
- **Neurological Exam:** Confirms right hemiparesis (face > arm > leg), right hemisensory loss (face > arm > leg), and right lower facial droop. Brisk reflexes on the right side suggest an upper motor neuron lesion.
- **Imaging:** CT scan shows an acute ischemic infarct in the *left* internal capsule.
- **Key Clue:** The combination of contralateral (right-sided) hemiparesis and hemisensory loss affecting the face, arm, and leg, with greater involvement of the face and arm than the leg, points to a lesion in a specific part of the brainstem or internal capsule. The CT localization to the *left* internal capsule is crucial.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Internal Capsule:** A white matter structure located deep within the cerebral hemispheres, separating the caudate nucleus and thalamus from the putamen and globus pallidus. It contains ascending sensory fibers (thalamocortical radiations) and descending motor fibers (corticospinal, corticobulbar tracts).
- **Anatomical Organization:** The internal capsule is typically divided into anterior, genu, posterior, retrolenticular, and sublenticular parts.
    - **Anterior Limb:** Contains thalamocortical fibers (sensory) and frontopontine fibers.
    - **Genu:** Contains corticobulbar fibers (motor to cranial nerves) and some thalamocortical fibers.
    - **Posterior Limb:** Contains corticospinal fibers (motor to body), thalamocortical fibers (sensory from body), and optic radiations.
    - **Retrolenticular Part:** Contains optic radiations (visual pathway).
    - **Sublenticular Part:** Contains auditory radiations (auditory pathway) and some thalamocortical fibers.
- **Clinical Correlation:**
    - A lesion in the *posterior limb* of the internal capsule affects the corticospinal tract (motor to body) and thalamocortical radiations (sensory from body). This typically causes contralateral hemiparesis (affecting face, arm, and leg relatively equally) and hemisensory loss (affecting face, arm, and leg relatively equally).
    - A lesion in the *genu* of the internal capsule affects the corticobulbar tract (motor to face and tongue) and some thalamocortical fibers. This typically causes contralateral weakness predominantly affecting the face and tongue (leading to facial droop, dysarthria, dysphagia) and potentially some sensory loss in the face. The arm and leg are relatively spared.
- **Vascular Supply:** The internal capsule is supplied by branches of the middle cerebral artery (MCA) and anterior cerebral artery (ACA). Infarcts in this region are common causes of stroke.
- **Patient's Presentation:** The patient has right-sided weakness and sensory loss affecting the face, arm, and leg, with the face and arm being *more* affected than the leg. This pattern, combined with dysarthria and dysphagia, strongly suggests a lesion affecting the corticobulbar fibers in the genu of the internal capsule, along with some adjacent sensory fibers. The CT scan confirms a lesion in the *left* internal capsule, which explains the *right-sided* deficits. Therefore, the genu of the internal capsule is the most likely location.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Putamen:** Part of the basal ganglia (lentiform nucleus). Primarily involved in motor control, learning, and habit formation. Lesions typically cause hypokinetic movement disorders (e.g., Parkinsonism if contralateral substantia nigra is also affected) or hyperkinetic disorders (e.g., chorea, dystonia). Does not contain major sensory or corticobulbar tracts. A lesion here would not cause the specific pattern of hemiparesis/hemisensory loss described.
- (B) **Globus Pallidus:** Another part of the basal ganglia (lentiform nucleus). Involved in motor control, particularly inhibiting movement. Lesions can cause hyperkinetic disorders (e.g., hemiballismus if contralateral subthalamic nucleus is affected). Does not contain major sensory or corticobulbar tracts. A lesion here would not cause the specific pattern of hemiparesis/hemisensory loss described.
- (C) **Substantia Nigra:** Located in the midbrain, part of the basal ganglia circuit. Degeneration of dopaminergic neurons here causes Parkinson's disease. Lesions typically cause Parkinsonism (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, postural instability). Does not contain major sensory or corticobulbar tracts. A lesion here would not cause the specific pattern of hemiparesis/hemisensory loss described.
- (E) **Caudate Nucleus:** Part of the basal ganglia. Involved in motor control, learning, memory, and emotion. Lesions can cause chorea (Huntington's disease), dystonia, or cognitive/behavioral changes. Does not contain major sensory or corticobulbar tracts. A lesion here would not cause the specific pattern of hemiparesis/hemisensory loss described.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location                 | Primary Function(s)                                  | Tracts Involved                                                                 | Clinical Manifestations of Lesion                                                                                                                               |
|----------------------|--------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Internal Capsule (Genu)** | Deep cerebrum, genu part | White matter tract relay                               | Corticobulbar (motor to face/tongue), some thalamocortical (sensory to face) | Contralateral weakness/sensory loss predominantly affecting face/tongue (dysarthria, dysphagia, facial droop), arm/leg relatively spared.                               |
| **Internal Capsule (Posterior Limb)** | Deep cerebrum, posterior part | White matter tract relay                               | Corticospinal (motor to body), thalamocortical (sensory from body)              | Contralateral hemiparesis (face, arm, leg relatively equally affected), hemisensory loss (face, arm, leg relatively equally affected).                               |
| Putamen              | Basal Ganglia (Lentiform) | Motor control, learning                               | None (part of basal ganglia circuit)                                            | Hypokinetic (Parkinsonism) or hyperkinetic (chorea, dystonia) movement disorders.                                                                                 |
| Globus Pallidus       | Basal Ganglia (Lentiform) | Motor control (inhibition)                             | None (part of basal ganglia circuit)                                            | Hyperkinetic movement disorders (e.g., hemiballismus).                                                                                                              |
| Substantia Nigra      | Midbrain                 | Dopamine production (motor control)                    | None (part of basal ganglia circuit)                                            | Parkinsonism (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor).                                                                                                                      |
| Caudate Nucleus       | Basal Ganglia            | Motor control, learning, cognition, emotion            | None (part of basal ganglia circuit)                                            | Chorea, dystonia, cognitive/behavioral changes.                                                                                                                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The internal capsule is a critical white matter pathway vulnerable to vascular events. Lesions in the genu typically affect corticobulbar fibers, causing contralateral facial/tongue weakness (dysarthria, dysphagia), while lesions in the posterior limb affect corticospinal fibers, causing contralateral body weakness. Understanding the specific tracts within each part of the internal capsule is key to localizing neurological deficits. Differentiating basal ganglia lesions (movement disorders) from internal capsule lesions (hemiparesis/hemisensory loss) is crucial.

<a id='question-124'></a>

### Question 124: Neuroscience: Basal Ganglia: Part 3 Focus 24 (through)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the anatomical structure and associated clinical syndrome resulting from a lesion affecting the globus pallidus interna (GPi) and its efferent pathways, specifically the hyperkinetic movement disorder known as hemiballismus. (识别损伤内侧苍白球 (GPi) 及其通路导致的解剖结构和相关临床综合征，特别是被称为半弹道运动的运动障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of hypertension and type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the emergency department with acute onset of involuntary, violent, flinging movements of his right arm and leg. The movements are proximal, affecting the shoulder and hip joints predominantly, and are described as "like being shot out of a cannon." He denies any recent trauma, fever, or changes in medication. Neurological examination reveals normal cranial nerves, strength, sensation, and reflexes. However, he exhibits the characteristic violent, proximal, involuntary movements on the right side of his body. A non-contrast head CT scan shows a small, well-defined hypodensity in the left globus pallidus interna (GPi). The patient is started on haloperidol, which significantly reduces the severity of the involuntary movements.

Which of the following anatomical structures is most likely the site of the primary lesion causing this patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Substantia nigra pars compacta
(B) Subthalamic nucleus
(C) Globus pallidus externa
(D) Caudate nucleus
(E) Ventral anterior nucleus of the thalamus

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with hypertension and diabetes. This suggests potential vascular risk factors.
- **Chief Complaint:** Acute onset of involuntary, violent, flinging movements of the right arm and leg. This is the hallmark description of hemiballismus.
- **Movement Characteristics:** Proximal, affecting shoulder and hip joints, described as "like being shot out of a cannon." This further confirms hemiballismus.
- **Neurological Examination:** Normal cranial nerves, strength, sensation, reflexes. This rules out many other neurological conditions. The key finding is the hemiballismus.
- **Imaging:** Non-contrast head CT shows a small, well-defined hypodensity in the *left* globus pallidus interna (GPi). This localizes the lesion.
- **Treatment Response:** Significant reduction of movements with haloperidol (a dopamine receptor antagonist). This suggests involvement of the dopaminergic pathways influencing the basal ganglia.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the anatomical structure most likely the site of the *primary* lesion.

The key clinical feature is hemiballismus, which is strongly associated with lesions of the subthalamic nucleus (STN) or its connections, particularly the GPi. The CT scan shows a lesion in the GPi. However, the classic cause of hemiballismus is a lesion of the contralateral STN. The question asks for the *primary* lesion site. While the CT shows a GPi lesion, the clinical syndrome (hemiballismus) is most classically associated with STN lesions. The STN normally exerts an excitatory influence on the GPi. Damage to the STN removes this excitation, leading to decreased activity in the GPi. The GPi normally inhibits the thalamus (specifically the ventral anterior nucleus, VA/VL). Decreased GPi activity leads to disinhibition of the thalamus, resulting in excessive thalamocortical excitation and the hyperkinetic movements of hemiballismus. A lesion *directly* in the GPi would cause *decreased* inhibition of the thalamus, leading to similar hyperkinetic movements (though sometimes described as choreoathetosis or dystonia rather than pure hemiballismus). However, the classic association and the most common cause of hemiballismus is STN damage. Given the options and the classic presentation, the STN is the most likely primary site. The CT finding in the GPi might represent secondary changes or an atypical presentation, but the clinical syndrome points strongly to the STN.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Basal Ganglia Circuitry:** The basal ganglia are involved in motor control, learning, and executive functions. The indirect pathway, relevant here, originates in the cortex, projects to the striatum (caudate and putamen), then to the GPe (globus pallidus externa), then to the STN (subthalamic nucleus), then to the GPi (globus pallidus interna), and finally to the thalamus (VA/VL nuclei), which projects back to the cortex.
- **Subthalamic Nucleus (STN):** Located ventral to the thalamus, medial to the GPi, and lateral to the substantia nigra. It receives input from the cortex and the GPe and projects excitatory glutamatergic fibers to the GPi and the substantia nigra pars reticulata (SNr). It is a key component of the indirect pathway.
- **Globus Pallidus Interna (GPi):** Located medial to the GPe and lateral to the thalamus. It receives inhibitory GABAergic input from the striatum (direct pathway) and excitatory glutamatergic input from the STN. It projects inhibitory GABAergic fibers to the thalamus (VA/VL) and the superior colliculus.
- **Hemiballismus:** A hyperkinetic movement disorder characterized by violent, involuntary, proximal limb movements, typically affecting one side of the body. It is most commonly caused by lesions affecting the STN or its projections, leading to disinhibition of the thalamus.
- **Vascular Supply:** The STN and GPi are supplied by branches of the posterior cerebral artery (PCA) and the posterior communicating artery (PCoA). Small vessel disease (lacunar infarcts) in patients with hypertension and diabetes is a common cause of STN lesions leading to hemiballismus.
- **Neurotransmitters:** The indirect pathway involves GABA (inhibitory) and Glutamate (excitatory). The STN uses glutamate to excite the GPi. The GPi uses GABA to inhibit the thalamus.
- **Pathophysiology of Hemiballismus:** Damage to the STN removes its excitatory drive to the GPi. This leads to decreased activity in the GPi. Consequently, the GPi provides less inhibition to the thalamus. The thalamus becomes overactive, leading to excessive excitation of the motor cortex and the resulting hyperkinetic movements.
- **Haloperidol:** A dopamine D2 receptor antagonist. While hemiballismus is classically associated with STN lesions (glutamatergic pathway), dopamine antagonists can sometimes provide symptomatic relief, possibly by modulating downstream pathways or having complex effects on basal ganglia circuitry.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Substantia nigra pars compacta:** This structure is primarily involved in dopamine production and projects to the striatum (nigrostriatal pathway). Lesions here cause Parkinson's disease, characterized by hypokinetic symptoms (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor), not hyperkinetic movements like hemiballismus.
- (C) **Globus pallidus externa:** The GPe is part of the indirect pathway and projects inhibitory GABAergic fibers to the STN. Lesions here typically cause less dramatic movement disorders, often involving chorea or dystonia, but not the classic violent flinging of hemiballismus. The CT finding was in the GPi, not the GPe.
- (D) **Caudate nucleus:** Part of the striatum, receives input from the cortex and projects to the GPe and GPi. Lesions here are associated with Huntington's disease (chorea) or various forms of dystonia, but not typically isolated hemiballismus.
- (E) **Ventral anterior nucleus of the thalamus:** This thalamic nucleus receives inhibitory input from the GPi and projects excitatory glutamatergic fibers to the motor cortex (via the corticospinal tract) and premotor cortex. Lesions here cause contralateral hemiparesis or weakness, not hyperkinetic movements. Damage *to* the thalamus (due to GPi lesion) causes hyperkinesia, but the primary lesion site causing hemiballismus is typically the STN or GPi. Given the classic association, STN is the best answer.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                                  | Primary Function in Basal Ganglia Circuitry | Clinical Syndrome Associated with Lesion                                  | Neurotransmitter(s) |
| :------------------------ | :---------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :----------------- |
| **Subthalamic Nucleus (STN)** | Ventral thalamus, medial GPi             | Excitatory input to GPi/SNr (Indirect Pathway) | **Hemiballismus** (most common cause)                                     | Glutamate          |
| Globus Pallidus Interna (GPi) | Medial GPe, lateral thalamus             | Inhibitory output to Thalamus/Superior Colliculus | Choreoathetosis, Dystonia (less common than STN for hemiballismus)        | GABA               |
| Substantia Nigra pars compacta | Midbrain, ventral striatum                | Dopaminergic input to Striatum (Direct/Indirect) | Parkinsonism (hypokinetic)                                                | Dopamine           |
| Globus Pallidus Externa (GPe) | Lateral GPi, medial Striatum             | Inhibitory input to STN (Indirect Pathway) | Chorea, Dystonia (less common)                                           | GABA               |
| Caudate Nucleus           | Part of Striatum, lateral ventricles      | Input from Cortex, output to GPe/GPi        | Huntington's Disease (Chorea), Dystonia                                  | GABA               |
| Ventral Anterior Thalamus | Thalamus, medial GPi                      | Output to Motor Cortex                       | Contralateral Hemiparesis/Weakness (lesion), Hyperkinesia (disinhibition) | Glutamate          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hemiballismus is a classic clinical syndrome strongly associated with lesions of the contralateral subthalamic nucleus (STN) or its projections, particularly the globus pallidus interna (GPi). The pathophysiology involves disinhibition of the thalamus due to loss of excitatory input from the STN to the GPi. Remember to differentiate hemiballismus (violent, proximal flinging) from chorea (dance-like, distal) and dystonia (sustained muscle contractions). While the CT showed a GPi lesion, the clinical syndrome points most strongly to the STN as the primary site.
